Oh, goodness... "afraid" might be a bit of a strong word. I really don't want to sound dramatic or anything.

Well, um... I wouldn't say I *restrict* exactly, that sounds so... deliberate. I just try to be careful, you know? 

I do a lot of the cooking at home. I make sure my husband and my mother-in-law have nice, full plates—they need their strength, of course. Well, um... I suppose it depends on what you mean by "significant," really. I don't want to make a fuss over nothing. 

My regular doctor, she seemed to think I've gotten too thin... and I suppose I have noticed my work pants are hanging a bit loose on me lately. I have to pull my belt a notch tighter than I used to. But... oh dear, I feel so embarrassed saying this out loud... part of me actually feels a little relieved when I see the number on the scale go down. 

I know I shouldn't feel that way, especially at my age. I should be more sensible, shouldn't I? I hope you don't think terribly of me for admitting that. Is it awful that I feel a bit glad about it?

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, um... I wouldn't say I look *overweight*, exactly. I know I'm not a big person. But... well, sometimes when I look in the mirror, especially if I've just eaten or if I feel a bit full, I just see... I don't know, it just looks wrong to me. Like my stomach is sticking out, or like I've just completely let myself go. 

I try to stay, you know, small and neat. When I'm tidy and put together, I just feel like I'm handling everything right—my job, the house, taking care of everyone. It's just... if I see any extra softness, it feels like a failure on my part. 

I know my doctor and my husband say I'm too thin, and I don't want to argue with them, I really don't. But my eyes just see something different sometimes. Does that make sense? I hope I don't sound completely foolish saying that out loud.

Just, you know, a little bit slipping off here and there from being so busy with the cleaning and running around. \n\nI haven't been keeping a chart or writing it down, so I'd hate to guess and give you the wrong answer. Is that alright?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I suppose it depends on what you mean by \"significant,\" really. I don't want to make a fuss over nothing. \n\nMy regular doctor, she seemed to think I've gotten too thin... and I suppose I have noticed my work pants are hanging a bit loose on me lately. I have to pull my belt a notch tighter than I used to. But... oh dear, I feel so embarrassed saying this out loud... part of me actually feels a little relieved when I see the number on the scale go down. \n\nI know I shouldn't feel that way, especially at my age. I should be more sensible, shouldn't I? I hope you don't think terribly of me for admitting that. Is it awful that I feel a bit glad about it?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look overweight even when others might say you're too thin?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I wouldn't say I look *overweight*, exactly. I know I'm not a big person. But... well, sometimes when I look in the mirror, especially if I've just eaten or if I feel a bit full, I just see... I don't know, it just looks wrong to me. Like my stomach is sticking out, or like I've just completely let myself go. \n\nI try to stay, you know, small and neat. When I'm tidy and put together, I just feel like I'm handling everything right—my job, the house, taking care of everyone. It's just... if I see any extra softness, it feels like a failure on my part. \n\nI know my doctor and my husband say I'm too thin, and I don't want to argue with them, I really don't. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, um... well, I wouldn't necessarily use the word "attractive." At my age, and working as a cleaner, I'm not exactly trying to be a glamour model or anything. I hope you don't think I'm that vain! 

But... I suppose, like I was just mentioning a moment ago, it really is tied to how I feel about my worth as a person. When I can just stay small and neat, I really do feel like I'm handling everything in my life right. It makes me feel disciplined and accomplished, I guess? 

But then... if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even the least bit full, I just get this awful, overwhelming feeling that I've completely let myself go. It's like all my hard work just vanishes and I'm failing at the most basic thing. I know that probably sounds terribly shallow of me to say... I really hope you don't judge me too harshly for thinking that way. Am I making any sense at all?

Oh, goodness... that's a very thoughtful question. I suppose... yes, I think I probably still would be anxious. Because, well... it's not just about the scale or the mirror. It's about knowing what I've done, if that makes sense?

Well, um... I haven't really thought about it exactly like that, to be honest. I hope that's an alright answer. 

I just... I don't really focus on the specific shape of my body, if that makes sense? I really don't want you to think I'm standing in front of the mirror analyzing my figure like a young girl might. It's more just... well, like I mentioned before, wanting to stay tidy and contained. 

I suppose if I gained weight, my shape would change, and I'm sure I wouldn't like the look of it, but... I don't think it's the shape itself that scares me so much. It's about knowing what I've done, if that makes sense? \n\nI feel a bit foolish admitting this, but... if I eat something a bit heavier, like, oh, maybe a biscuit with my tea or a bit of pasta, I just spend the rest of the day feeling so scared that it's going to show on me. Even if I couldn't look in a mirror, I'd still just *know* it was there. So I end up constantly checking myself anyway, just feeling my waist or my stomach, and thinking I need to scrub the floors harder on my next shift or skip my next meal just to make up for it. \n\nI know I shouldn't let a simple biscuit upset me so much at my age. I should know better by now, shouldn't I? I hope you don't think I'm completely neurotic for behaving that way. Did that answer your question alright? I want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because you believe it will change your body shape in a way that you won't like?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I haven't really thought about it exactly like that, to be honest. I hope that's an alright answer. \n\nI just... I don't really focus on the specific shape of my body, if that makes sense? I really don't want you to think I'm standing in front of the mirror analyzing my figure like a young girl might. It's more just... well, like I mentioned before, wanting to stay tidy and contained. \n\nI suppose if I gained weight, my shape would change, and I'm sure I wouldn't like the look of it, but... I don't think it's the shape itself that scares me so much. It's more just the thought of taking up more space, or feeling like I've lost my discipline. I'm sorry, I feel like I might just be talking in circles now. Am I helping at all with these answers? I really do want to give you what you need.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your weight gain would change the way you perceive your body?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 64 / janitor_or_building_cleaner / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance\nmanifestation: \"When I stay small and neat, I feel like I'm handling everything right, but if my stomach looks the least bit full in the mirror I feel like I've let myself go.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, um... well, yes, I suppose it would. I think I might have touched on this a little bit earlier, actually, but... yes, it absolutely would change how I see myself. 

It's just... well, like I said, when I can stay small and neat, I really feel like I'm handling everything right. I look at myself and I see someone who is disciplined and, I don't know, put-together? But if I were to gain weight... oh dear. If I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even the least bit full, I just get this dreadful feeling that I've completely let myself go. It changes from seeing someone who is trying her best to seeing someone who has just... failed. 

I know it sounds so silly to let my stomach dictate how I see my whole self. I really hope you don't think I'm terribly superficial for admitting that. I just want to be honest with you so you can do your job. Is that the sort of thing you were asking about?

I really hope you don't think I'm terribly superficial for admitting that. I just want to be honest with you so you can do your job. Is that the sort of thing you were asking about?\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you have a specific weight in mind that you try to maintain?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, um... a specific weight? Well, no, not exactly. I don't really have a strict number in my head that I'm trying to hit. I hope that's an okay answer... I know some people have very specific goals. 

For me, it's more just about... well, feeling right. I just want to make sure I don't get too heavy, you know? As long as my clothes still fit nicely and I feel light enough to get through my shifts at work without feeling sluggish... that's what matters most to me. 

I suppose I do check the scale to make sure the number isn't creeping up, but it's not like I have a magic number I'm obsessed with. I just try to keep things steady and manageable. I hope I'm making sense? I'd hate for you to think I'm being evasive, I just really don't have a specific number to give you.

I know how silly that sounds for a woman my age. I don't want to make a fuss over nothing. \n\nMy regular doctor, she seemed to think I've gotten too thin... and I suppose I have noticed my work pants are hanging a bit loose on me lately. But... oh dear, I feel so embarrassed saying this out loud... part of me actually feels a little relieved when I see the number on the scale go down. \n\nI know I shouldn't feel that way, especially at my age. I should be more sensible, shouldn't I? I hope you don't think terribly of me for admitting that. Is it awful that I feel a bit glad about it?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look overweight even when others might say you're too thin?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I wouldn't say I look *overweight*, exactly. I know I'm not a big person. But... well, sometimes when I look in the mirror, especially if I've just eaten or if I feel a bit full, I just see... Well, um... I suppose it is connected, yes. I think I might have said something a bit like this just a moment ago, but... well, it's just that when I can stay small and neat, I really do feel like I'm handling everything right. Like I'm in control of things.

I really don't want to sound dramatic or anything. \n\nBut... well, I suppose if I'm being completely honest, yes, a little bit. It's just... if I eat something a bit heavier, like, oh, some pasta or a biscuit, I just... I spend the rest of the day feeling so scared that it's going to show on me right away. I know how silly that sounds for a woman my age. I feel a bit ashamed to even admit it, but I'll catch myself checking the mirror, and I just get this thought stuck in my head that I need to make up for it somehow. \n\nI hope I don't sound completely ridiculous telling you that... is that the sort of thing you meant?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever restrict what you eat because of this fear?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I wouldn't say I *restrict* exactly, that sounds so... deliberate. I just try to be careful, you know? \n\nI do a lot of the cooking at home. I make sure my husband and my mother-in-law have nice, full plates—they need their strength, of course. But... well, by the time I actually sit down with them, I just... I usually tell myself that a cup of coffee, or maybe just a few vegetables or half a piece of toast, is really enough for me. \n\nI'm on my feet so much at work, I just don't have a huge appetite anyway. And it's better to eat light than to feel all weighed down, right? I hope that doesn't sound like I'm doing something wrong. Is it okay to just eat a little bit if you're not hungry?\n\nDoctor: How much weight have you lost recently?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... I'm actually not entirely sure of the exact number. I hope it's okay that I don't have a specific amount to give you. \n\nI don't really keep a strict record of it or anything... I just know my regular doctor said it was enough for her to want me to come see you. But to me, honestly, it really hasn't felt like all that much. Just, you know, a little bit slipping off here and there from being so busy with the cleaning and running around. \n\nI haven't been keeping a chart or writing it down, so I'd hate to guess and give you the wrong answer. Is that alright?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I suppose it depends on what you mean by \"significant,\" really. I don't want to make a fuss over nothing. \n\nMy regular doctor, she seemed to think I've gotten too thin... and I suppose I have noticed my work pants are hanging a bit loose on me lately. I have to pull my belt a notch tighter than I used to. But... oh dear, I feel so embarrassed saying this out loud... part of me actually feels a little relieved when I see the number on the scale go down. \n\nI know I shouldn't feel that way, especially at my age. I should be more sensible, shouldn't I? I hope you don't think terribly of me for admitting that. Is it awful that I feel a bit glad about it?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look overweight even when others might say you're too thin?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I wouldn't say I look *overweight*, exactly. I know I'm not a big person. But... well, sometimes when I look in the mirror, especially if I've just eaten or if I feel a bit full, I just see... I don't know, it just looks wrong to me. Like my stomach is sticking out, or like I've just completely let myself go. \n\nI try to stay, you know, small and neat. When I'm tidy and put together, I just feel like I'm handling everything right—my job, the house, taking care of everyone. It's just... if I see any extra softness, it feels like a failure on my part. \n\nI know my doctor and my husband say I'm too thin, and I don't want to argue with them, I really don't. But my eyes just see something different sometimes. Does that make sense? I hope I don't sound completely foolish saying that out loud.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the only thing that determines whether you are attractive or not?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I wouldn't necessarily use the word \"attractive.\" At my age, and working as a cleaner, I'm not exactly trying to be a glamour model or anything. I hope you don't think I'm that vain! \n\nBut... I suppose, like I was just mentioning a moment ago, it really is tied to how I feel about my worth as a person. When I can just stay small and neat, I really do feel like I'm handling everything in my life right. It makes me feel disciplined and accomplished, I guess? \n\nBut then... if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even the least bit full, I just get this awful, overwhelming feeling that I've completely let myself go. It's like all my hard work just vanishes and I'm failing at the most basic thing. I know that probably sounds terribly shallow of me to say... I really hope you don't judge me too harshly for thinking that way. Am I making any sense at all?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious about gaining weight if you couldn't see your body or weigh yourself?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness... that's a very thoughtful question. I suppose... yes, I think I probably still would be anxious. Because, well... it's not just about the scale or the mirror. It's about knowing what I've done, if that makes sense? \n\nI feel a bit foolish admitting this, but... if I eat something a bit heavier, like, oh, maybe a biscuit with my tea or a bit of pasta, I just spend the rest of the day feeling so scared that it's going to show on me. Even if I couldn't look in a mirror, I'd still just *know* it was there. So I end up constantly checking myself anyway, just feeling my waist or my stomach, and thinking I need to scrub the floors harder on my next shift or skip my next meal just to make up for it. \n\nI know I shouldn't let a simple biscuit upset me so much at my age. I should know better by now, shouldn't I? I hope you don't think I'm completely neurotic for behaving that way. Did that answer your question alright? I want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because you believe it will change your body shape in a way that you won't like?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I haven't really thought about it exactly like that, to be honest. I hope that's an alright answer. \n\nI just... I don't really focus on the specific shape of my body, if that makes sense? I really don't want you to think I'm standing in front of the mirror analyzing my figure like a young girl might. It's more just... well, like I mentioned before, wanting to stay tidy and contained. \n\nI suppose if I gained weight, my shape would change, and I'm sure I wouldn't like the look of it, but... I don't think it's the shape itself that scares me so much. It's more just the thought of taking up more space, or feeling like I've lost my discipline. I'm sorry, I feel like I might just be talking in circles now. Am I helping at all with these answers? I hope I don't sound completely foolish saying that out loud.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the only thing that determines whether you are attractive or not?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I wouldn't necessarily use the word \"attractive.\" At my age, and working as a cleaner, I'm not exactly trying to be a glamour model or anything. I hope you don't think I'm that vain! \n\nBut... I suppose, like I was just mentioning a moment ago, it really is tied to how I feel about my worth as a person. When I can just stay small and neat, I really do feel like I'm handling everything in my life right. It makes me feel disciplined and accomplished, I guess? \n\nBut then... if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even the least bit full, I just get this awful, overwhelming feeling that I've completely let myself go. It's like all my hard work just vanishes and I'm failing at the most basic thing. I know that probably sounds terribly shallow of me to say... I really hope you don't judge me too harshly for thinking that way. Am I making any sense at all?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious about gaining weight if you couldn't see your body or weigh yourself?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness... that's a very thoughtful question. I suppose... yes, I think I probably still would be anxious. Because, well... it's not just about the scale or the mirror. It's about knowing what I've done, if that makes sense? \n\nI feel a bit foolish admitting this, but... if I eat something a bit heavier, like, oh, maybe a biscuit with my tea or a bit of pasta, I just spend the rest of the day feeling so scared that it's going to show on me. Even if I couldn't look in a mirror, I'd still just *know* it was there. So I end up constantly checking myself anyway, just feeling my waist or my stomach, and thinking I need to scrub the floors harder on my next shift or skip my next meal just to make up for it. \n\nI know I shouldn't let a simple biscuit upset me so much at my age. I should know better by now, shouldn't I? I hope you don't think I'm completely neurotic for behaving that way. Did that answer your question alright? I want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because you believe it will change your body shape in a way that you won't like?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I haven't really thought about it exactly like that, to be honest. I hope that's an alright answer. \n\nI just... I don't really focus on the specific shape of my body, if that makes sense? I really don't want you to think I'm standing in front of the mirror analyzing my figure like a young girl might. It's more just... well, like I mentioned before, wanting to stay tidy and contained. \n\nI suppose if I gained weight, my shape would change, and I'm sure I wouldn't like the look of it, but... I don't think it's the shape itself that scares me so much. It's more just the thought of taking up more space, or feeling like I've lost my discipline. I'm sorry, I feel like I might just be talking in circles now. Am I helping at all with these answers? If I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even the least bit full, I just get this dreadful feeling that I've completely let myself go. It changes from seeing someone who is trying her best to seeing someone who has just... failed. \n\nI know it sounds so silly to let my stomach dictate how I see my whole self. I really hope you don't think I'm terribly superficial for admitting that. I just want to be honest with you so you can do your job. Is that the sort of thing you were asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you have a specific weight in mind that you try to maintain?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... a specific weight? Well, no, not exactly. I don't really have a strict number in my head that I'm trying to hit. I hope that's an okay answer... I know some people have very specific goals. \n\nFor me, it's more just about... well, feeling right. I just want to make sure I don't get too heavy, you know? As long as my clothes still fit nicely and I feel light enough to get through my shifts at work without feeling sluggish... that's what matters most to me. \n\nI suppose I do check the scale to make sure the number isn't creeping up, but it's not like I have a magic number I'm obsessed with. I just try to keep things steady and manageable. I hope I'm making sense? I'd hate for you to think I'm being evasive, I just really don't have a specific number to give you.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like being in control of your eating and weight is connected to being in control of other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I suppose it is connected, yes. I think I might have said something a bit like this just a moment ago, but... well, it's just that when I can stay small and neat, I really do feel like I'm handling everything right. Like I'm in control of things. \n\nBut if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even the least bit full, I just... I get this awful feeling that I've let myself go. And then it feels like everything else is slipping, too. It's silly, isn't it? Letting something so small make me feel like I'm failing at everything. Oh, um... if I couldn't see myself? That's an interesting question. I've never really thought about it quite like that before. 

I suppose... well, maybe I wouldn't feel quite as anxious? I'm honestly not entirely sure. I think so much of it is tied to what I see in the mirror, like I mentioned before with my stomach. If I couldn't see that, maybe I wouldn't have that immediate trigger that makes me feel like I've failed.

Oh, um... well, yes, I suppose it does feel a bit like that. I really try not to let myself get too worked up, but... 

Well, if I happen to eat something a little heavier—like, oh, maybe a biscuit with my tea, or a bit of pasta—I just... I wouldn't say I *restrict* exactly, that sounds so... deliberate. I just try to be careful, you know? \n\nI do a lot of the cooking at home. I make sure my husband and my mother-in-law have nice, full plates—they need their strength, of course. But... well, by the time I actually sit down with them, I just... I usually tell myself that a cup of coffee, or maybe just a few vegetables or half a piece of toast, is really enough for me. \n\nI'm on my feet so much at work, I just don't have a huge appetite anyway. And it's better to eat light than to feel all weighed down, right? I hope that doesn't sound like I'm doing something wrong. Is it okay to just eat a little bit if you're not hungry?\n\nDoctor: How much weight have you lost recently?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... I'm actually not entirely sure of the exact number. I hope it's okay that I don't have a specific amount to give you. \n\nI don't really keep a strict record of it or anything... I just know my regular doctor said it was enough for her to want me to come see you. But to me, honestly, it really hasn't felt like all that much. Just, you know, a little bit slipping off here and there from being so busy with the cleaning and running around. \n\nI haven't been keeping a chart or writing it down, so I'd hate to guess and give you the wrong answer. Is that alright?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I suppose it depends on what you mean by \"significant,\" really. I don't want to make a fuss over nothing. \n\nMy regular doctor, she seemed to think I've gotten too thin... and I suppose I have noticed my work pants are hanging a bit loose on me lately. I have to pull my belt a notch tighter than I used to. But... oh dear, I feel so embarrassed saying this out loud... part of me actually feels a little relieved when I see the number on the scale go down. \n\nI know I shouldn't feel that way, especially at my age. I should be more sensible, shouldn't I? I hope you don't think terribly of me for admitting that. Is it awful that I feel a bit glad about it?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look overweight even when others might say you're too thin?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I wouldn't say I look *overweight*, exactly. I know I'm not a big person. But... well, sometimes when I look in the mirror, especially if I've just eaten or if I feel a bit full, I just see... I don't know, it just looks wrong to me. Like my stomach is sticking out, or like I've just completely let myself go. \n\nI try to stay, you know, small and neat. When I'm tidy and put together, I just feel like I'm handling everything right—my job, the house, taking care of everyone. It's just... if I see any extra softness, it feels like a failure on my part. \n\nI know my doctor and my husband say I'm too thin, and I don't want to argue with them, I really don't. But my eyes just see something different sometimes. Does that make sense? I hope I don't sound completely foolish saying that out loud.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the only thing that determines whether you are attractive or not?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I wouldn't necessarily use the word \"attractive.\" At my age, and working as a cleaner, I'm not exactly trying to be a glamour model or anything. I hope you don't think I'm that vain! \n\nBut... I suppose, like I was just mentioning a moment ago, it really is tied to how I feel about my worth as a person. When I can just stay small and neat, I really do feel like I'm handling everything in my life right. It makes me feel disciplined and accomplished, I guess? \n\nBut then... if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even the least bit full, I just get this awful, overwhelming feeling that I've completely let myself go. It's like all my hard work just vanishes and I'm failing at the most basic thing. I know that probably sounds terribly shallow of me to say... I really hope you don't judge me too harshly for thinking that way. Am I making any sense at all?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious about gaining weight if you couldn't see your body or weigh yourself?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness... that's a very thoughtful question. I suppose... yes, I think I probably still would be anxious. Because, well... it's not just about the scale or the mirror. It's about knowing what I've done, if that makes sense? \n\nI feel a bit foolish admitting this, but... if I eat something a bit heavier, like, oh, maybe a biscuit with my tea or a bit of pasta, I just spend the rest of the day feeling so scared that it's going to show on me. Even if I couldn't look in a mirror, I'd still just *know* it was there. So I end up constantly checking myself anyway, just feeling my waist or my stomach, and thinking I need to scrub the floors harder on my next shift or skip my next meal just to make up for it. \n\nI know I shouldn't let a simple biscuit upset me so much at my age. I should know better by now, shouldn't I? I hope you don't think I'm completely neurotic for behaving that way. Did that answer your question alright? I want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because you believe it will change your body shape in a way that you won't like?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I haven't really thought about it exactly like that, to be honest. I hope that's an alright answer. \n\nI just... I don't really focus on the specific shape of my body, if that makes sense? I really don't want you to think I'm standing in front of the mirror analyzing my figure like a young girl might. It's more just... well, like I mentioned before, wanting to stay tidy and contained. \n\nI suppose if I gained weight, my shape would change, and I'm sure I wouldn't like the look of it, but... I don't think it's the shape itself that scares me so much. It's more just the thought of taking up more space, or feeling like I've lost my discipline. I'm sorry, I feel like I might just be talking in circles now. Am I helping at all with these answers? I really do want to give you what you need.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight gain would change the way you perceive your body?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, yes, I suppose it would. I think I might have touched on this a little bit earlier, actually, but... yes, it absolutely would change how I see myself. \n\nIt's just... well, like I said, when I can stay small and neat, I really feel like I'm handling everything right. I look at myself and I see someone who is disciplined and, I don't know, put-together? But if I were to gain weight... oh dear. If I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even the least bit full, I just get this dreadful feeling that I've completely let myself go. It changes from seeing someone who is trying her best to seeing someone who has just... failed. \n\nI know it sounds so silly to let my stomach dictate how I see my whole self. I really hope you don't think I'm terribly superficial for admitting that. I just want to be honest with you so you can do your job. Is that the sort of thing you were asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you have a specific weight in mind that you try to maintain?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... a specific weight? Well, no, not exactly. I don't really have a strict number in my head that I'm trying to hit. I hope that's an okay answer... I know some people have very specific goals. \n\nFor me, it's more just about... well, feeling right. I just want to make sure I don't get too heavy, you know? As long as my clothes still fit nicely and I feel light enough to get through my shifts at work without feeling sluggish... that's what matters most to me. \n\nI suppose I do check the scale to make sure the number isn't creeping up, but it's not like I have a magic number I'm obsessed with. I just try to keep things steady and manageable. I hope I'm making sense? I'd hate for you to think I'm being evasive, I just really don't have a specific number to give you.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like being in control of your eating and weight is connected to being in control of other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I suppose it is connected, yes. I think I might have said something a bit like this just a moment ago, but... well, it's just that when I can stay small and neat, I really do feel like I'm handling everything right. Like I'm in control of things. \n\nBut if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even the least bit full, I just... I get this awful feeling that I've let myself go. And then it feels like everything else is slipping, too. It's silly, isn't it? Letting something so small make me feel like I'm failing at everything. I try not to let it get to me too much, but... well, it's hard. \n\nDoes that answer your question? I want to make sure I'm giving you the right kind of information. I'm really trying my best to explain it properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious about your body if you couldn't see yourself?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... if I couldn't see myself? That's an interesting question. I've never really thought about it quite like that before. \n\nI suppose... well, maybe I wouldn't feel quite as anxious? I'm honestly not entirely sure. I think so much of it is tied to what I see in the mirror, like I mentioned before with my stomach. If I couldn't see that, maybe I wouldn't have that immediate trigger that makes me feel like I've failed. \n\nBut then again, I still have to wear my clothes and go to work, and I can feel how my uniform fits, you know? So I might still worry about feeling heavy or sluggish. I really don't know for certain, though. I'm sorry I can't give you a more definite answer. Is it okay that I'm not entirely sure about that one? I hope I'm still being helpful.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like gaining weight would mean you're losing control of your life?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, yes, I suppose it does feel a bit like that. I really try not to let myself get too worked up, but... \n\nWell, if I happen to eat something a little heavier—like, oh, maybe a biscuit with my tea, or a bit of pasta—I just... I spend the rest of the day feeling so frightened that it's going to show on me right away. I know it's probably silly, but I find myself constantly checking my reflection in the windows or the mirrors while I'm cleaning the building, just to make sure I haven't suddenly puffed up. \n\nAnd then I get this overwhelming thought that I need to... well, make up for it somehow. Maybe work a little harder on my feet, or just be extra careful the next day. \n\nI hope you don't judge me too harshly for that. I know a woman my age shouldn't be so terribly fussed about a biscuit, but it just really scares me to feel like I'm losing my grip on things. Does that answer what you were asking? I want to make sure I'm giving you what you need.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, goodness... no, I wouldn't say it's the *only* part. I mean, I do have my job, and I take a lot of pride in keeping the building nice and tidy. I like knowing that I'm doing a good job there, and I feel quite capable when I'm working.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello. Thank you so much for having me. I, um, I hope I'm explaining this right. \n\nMy regular doctor actually sent me here. She seems to think I've gotten a little too thin. I mean, I clean office buildings for a living, so I'm always on my feet, and I guess I can see that my work pants are hanging pretty loose on me lately. But... oh, I feel a little embarrassed to even say this... part of me still feels, well, relieved when the number on the scale goes down. \n\nI know I probably shouldn't feel that way at my age, and I don't want to cause any fuss, but... does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness... \"afraid\" might be a bit of a strong word. I really don't want to sound dramatic or anything. \n\nBut... well, I suppose if I'm being completely honest, yes, a little bit. It's just... if I eat something a bit heavier, like, oh, some pasta or a biscuit, I just... I spend the rest of the day feeling so scared that it's going to show on me right away. I know how silly that sounds for a woman my age. I feel a bit ashamed to even admit it, but I'll catch myself checking the mirror, and I just get this thought stuck in my head that I need to make up for it somehow. \n\nI hope I don't sound completely ridiculous telling you that... is that the sort of thing you meant?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever restrict what you eat because of this fear?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I wouldn't say I *restrict* exactly, that sounds so... deliberate. I just try to be careful, you know? \n\nI do a lot of the cooking at home. I make sure my husband and my mother-in-law have nice, full plates—they need their strength, of course. But... well, by the time I actually sit down with them, I just... I usually tell myself that a cup of coffee, or maybe just a few vegetables or half a piece of toast, is really enough for me. \n\nI'm on my feet so much at work, I just don't have a huge appetite anyway. And it's better to eat light than to feel all weighed down, right? I hope that doesn't sound like I'm doing something wrong. Is it okay to just eat a little bit if you're not hungry?\n\nDoctor: How much weight have you lost recently?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... I'm actually not entirely sure of the exact number. I hope it's okay that I don't have a specific amount to give you. \n\nI don't really keep a strict record of it or anything... I just know my regular doctor said it was enough for her to want me to come see you. But to me, honestly, it really hasn't felt like all that much. Just, you know, a little bit slipping off here and there from being so busy with the cleaning and running around. \n\nI haven't been keeping a chart or writing it down, so I'd hate to guess and give you the wrong answer. Is that alright?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I suppose it depends on what you mean by \"significant,\" really. I don't want to make a fuss over nothing. \n\nMy regular doctor, she seemed to think I've gotten too thin... and I suppose I have noticed my work pants are hanging a bit loose on me lately. I have to pull my belt a notch tighter than I used to. But... oh dear, I feel so embarrassed saying this out loud... part of me actually feels a little relieved when I see the number on the scale go down. \n\nI know I shouldn't feel that way, especially at my age. I should be more sensible, shouldn't I? I hope you don't think terribly of me for admitting that. Is it awful that I feel a bit glad about it?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look overweight even when others might say you're too thin?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I wouldn't say I look *overweight*, exactly. I know I'm not a big person. But... well, sometimes when I look in the mirror, especially if I've just eaten or if I feel a bit full, I just see... I don't know, it just looks wrong to me. Like my stomach is sticking out, or like I've just completely let myself go. \n\nI try to stay, you know, small and neat. When I'm tidy and put together, I just feel like I'm handling everything right—my job, the house, taking care of everyone. It's just... if I see any extra softness, it feels like a failure on my part. \n\nI know my doctor and my husband say I'm too thin, and I don't want to argue with them, I really don't. But my eyes just see something different sometimes. Does that make sense? I hope I don't sound completely foolish saying that out loud.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the only thing that determines whether you are attractive or not?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I wouldn't necessarily use the word \"attractive.\" At my age, and working as a cleaner, I'm not exactly trying to be a glamour model or anything. I hope you don't think I'm that vain! \n\nBut... I suppose, like I was just mentioning a moment ago, it really is tied to how I feel about my worth as a person. When I can just stay small and neat, I really do feel like I'm handling everything in my life right. It makes me feel disciplined and accomplished, I guess? \n\nBut then... if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even the least bit full, I just get this awful, overwhelming feeling that I've completely let myself go. It's like all my hard work just vanishes and I'm failing at the most basic thing. I know that probably sounds terribly shallow of me to say... I really hope you don't judge me too harshly for thinking that way. Am I making any sense at all?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious about gaining weight if you couldn't see your body or weigh yourself?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness... that's a very thoughtful question. I suppose... yes, I think I probably still would be anxious. Because, well... it's not just about the scale or the mirror. It's about knowing what I've done, if that makes sense? \n\nI feel a bit foolish admitting this, but... if I eat something a bit heavier, like, oh, maybe a biscuit with my tea or a bit of pasta, I just spend the rest of the day feeling so scared that it's going to show on me. Even if I couldn't look in a mirror, I'd still just *know* it was there. So I end up constantly checking myself anyway, just feeling my waist or my stomach, and thinking I need to scrub the floors harder on my next shift or skip my next meal just to make up for it. \n\nI know I shouldn't let a simple biscuit upset me so much at my age. I should know better by now, shouldn't I? I hope you don't think I'm completely neurotic for behaving that way. Did that answer your question alright? I want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because you believe it will change your body shape in a way that you won't like?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I haven't really thought about it exactly like that, to be honest. I hope that's an alright answer. \n\nI just... I don't really focus on the specific shape of my body, if that makes sense? I really don't want you to think I'm standing in front of the mirror analyzing my figure like a young girl might. It's more just... well, like I mentioned before, wanting to stay tidy and contained. \n\nI suppose if I gained weight, my shape would change, and I'm sure I wouldn't like the look of it, but... I don't think it's the shape itself that scares me so much. It's more just the thought of taking up more space, or feeling like I've lost my discipline. I'm sorry, I feel like I might just be talking in circles now. Am I helping at all with these answers? I really do want to give you what you need.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight gain would change the way you perceive your body?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, yes, I suppose it would. I think I might have touched on this a little bit earlier, actually, but... yes, it absolutely would change how I see myself. \n\nIt's just... well, like I said, when I can stay small and neat, I really feel like I'm handling everything right. I look at myself and I see someone who is disciplined and, I don't know, put-together? But if I were to gain weight... oh dear. If I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even the least bit full, I just get this dreadful feeling that I've completely let myself go. It changes from seeing someone who is trying her best to seeing someone who has just... failed. \n\nI know it sounds so silly to let my stomach dictate how I see my whole self. I really hope you don't think I'm terribly superficial for admitting that. I just want to be honest with you so you can do your job. Is that the sort of thing you were asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you have a specific weight in mind that you try to maintain?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... a specific weight? Well, no, not exactly. I don't really have a strict number in my head that I'm trying to hit. I hope that's an okay answer... I know some people have very specific goals. \n\nFor me, it's more just about... well, feeling right. I just want to make sure I don't get too heavy, you know? As long as my clothes still fit nicely and I feel light enough to get through my shifts at work without feeling sluggish... that's what matters most to me. \n\nI suppose I do check the scale to make sure the number isn't creeping up, but it's not like I have a magic number I'm obsessed with. I just try to keep things steady and manageable. I hope I'm making sense? I'd hate for you to think I'm being evasive, I just really don't have a specific number to give you.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like being in control of your eating and weight is connected to being in control of other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I suppose it is connected, yes. I want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because you believe it will change your body shape in a way that you won't like?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I haven't really thought about it exactly like that, to be honest. I hope that's an alright answer. \n\nI just... I don't really focus on the specific shape of my body, if that makes sense? I really don't want you to think I'm standing in front of the mirror analyzing my figure like a young girl might. It's more just... well, like I mentioned before, wanting to stay tidy and contained. \n\nI suppose if I gained weight, my shape would change, and I'm sure I wouldn't like the look of it, but... I don't think it's the shape itself that scares me so much. It's more just the thought of taking up more space, or feeling like I've lost my discipline. I'm sorry, I feel like I might just be talking in circles now. Am I helping at all with these answers? I really do want to give you what you need.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight gain would change the way you perceive your body?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, yes, I suppose it would. I think I might have touched on this a little bit earlier, actually, but... yes, it absolutely would change how I see myself. \n\nIt's just... well, like I said, when I can stay small and neat, I really feel like I'm handling everything right. I look at myself and I see someone who is disciplined and, I don't know, put-together? But if I were to gain weight... oh dear. If I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even the least bit full, I just get this dreadful feeling that I've completely let myself go. It changes from seeing someone who is trying her best to seeing someone who has just... failed. \n\nI know it sounds so silly to let my stomach dictate how I see my whole self. I really hope you don't think I'm terribly superficial for admitting that. I just want to be honest with you so you can do your job. Is that the sort of thing you were asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you have a specific weight in mind that you try to maintain?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... a specific weight? Well, no, not exactly. I don't really have a strict number in my head that I'm trying to hit. I hope that's an okay answer... I know some people have very specific goals. \n\nFor me, it's more just about... well, feeling right. I just want to make sure I don't get too heavy, you know? As long as my clothes still fit nicely and I feel light enough to get through my shifts at work without feeling sluggish... that's what matters most to me. \n\nI suppose I do check the scale to make sure the number isn't creeping up, but it's not like I have a magic number I'm obsessed with. I just try to keep things steady and manageable. I hope I'm making sense? I'd hate for you to think I'm being evasive, I just really don't have a specific number to give you.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like being in control of your eating and weight is connected to being in control of other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Well, um... It changes from seeing someone who is trying her best to seeing someone who has just... failed. \n\nI know it sounds so silly to let my stomach dictate how I see my whole self. Is that the sort of thing you were asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you have a specific weight in mind that you try to maintain?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... a specific weight? Well, no, not exactly. I don't really have a strict number in my head that I'm trying to hit. I hope that's an okay answer... As long as my clothes still fit nicely and I feel light enough to get through my shifts at work without feeling sluggish... that's what matters most to me. \n\nI suppose I do check the scale to make sure the number isn't creeping up, but it's not like I have a magic number I'm obsessed with. I just try to keep things steady and manageable. I hope I'm making sense? I'd hate for you to think I'm being evasive, I just really don't have a specific number to give you.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like being in control of your eating and weight is connected to being in control of other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I know a woman my age shouldn't be so terribly fussed about a biscuit, but it just really scares me to feel like I'm losing my grip on things. Does that answer what you were asking? I want to make sure I'm giving you what you need.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your weight is the only part of your life where you feel in control?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness... no, I wouldn't say it's the *only* part. I mean, I do have my job, and I take a lot of pride in keeping the building nice and tidy. I like knowing that I'm doing a good job there, and I feel quite capable when I'm working. \n\nBut... well, I suppose when it comes to myself, personally... the eating and the weight do feel like the main things I can really manage. Everything else just sort of happens around me, you know? But what I put on my plate, or how my uniform fits... that's just me. I can handle that. \n\nI don't mean to make it sound like my whole life is a mess otherwise! I really do try to keep things orderly. I just... I suppose I rely on the weight thing a bit more than I probably should. Is that the sort of thing you were wondering about? I hope I'm explaining it alright.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because you have a distorted view of what you look like?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... a distorted view? I... I'm not really sure I'd call it distorted. I mean, I know I'm not a large woman, I really do. \n\nBut... well, I suppose it's just that when I look in the mirror and I see that I've stayed small and neat, it just makes me feel like I'm handling everything right, you know? Like I'm keeping myself proper. But if I happen to catch my reflection and my stomach looks even the least bit full... oh, it just makes me feel dreadful. I feel like I've completely let myself go, and I just can't bear that thought. \n\nI know it probably sounds a bit silly to worry so much about a tiny bit of fullness, especially at my age. I'm sorry if it seems foolish. But to me, it just feels so real in that moment. Do you think that means my view is distorted? I really want to understand what you think about it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like gaining weight would change how others perceive you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 64 / janitor_or_building_cleaner / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, um... how others perceive me? Well, goodness, I haven't really thought about it quite like that. I suppose... no, not really. I mean, most of the people in the building are so busy with their own work, I don't think they really look twice at the cleaning lady, if I'm being perfectly honest. 

I hope that doesn't sound pitiful! I just mean... I always make sure my uniform is pressed and I look tidy, of course, because I want to be professional and make a good impression. But as for them noticing if I gained a little weight... I really don't think they'd mind or treat me any differently at all. My worrying is usually just between me and the mirror, I suppose. 

Is that okay? I hope I'm not misunderstanding what you were asking. I just want to be sure I'm giving you the right sort of answer.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello. Thank you so much for having me. I, um, I hope I'm explaining this right. \n\nMy regular doctor actually sent me here. She seems to think I've gotten a little too thin. I mean, I clean office buildings for a living, so I'm always on my feet, and I guess I can see that my work pants are hanging pretty loose on me lately. But... oh, I feel a little embarrassed to even say this... part of me still feels, well, relieved when the number on the scale goes down. \n\nI know I probably shouldn't feel that way at my age, and I don't want to cause any fuss, but... does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness... \"afraid\" might be a bit of a strong word. I really don't want to sound dramatic or anything. \n\nBut... well, I suppose if I'm being completely honest, yes, a little bit. It's just... if I eat something a bit heavier, like, oh, some pasta or a biscuit, I just... I spend the rest of the day feeling so scared that it's going to show on me right away. I know how silly that sounds for a woman my age. I feel a bit ashamed to even admit it, but I'll catch myself checking the mirror, and I just get this thought stuck in my head that I need to make up for it somehow. \n\nI hope I don't sound completely ridiculous telling you that... is that the sort of thing you meant?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever restrict what you eat because of this fear?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I wouldn't say I *restrict* exactly, that sounds so... deliberate. I just try to be careful, you know? \n\nI do a lot of the cooking at home. I usually tell myself that a cup of coffee, or maybe just a few vegetables or half a piece of toast, is really enough for me. \n\nI'm on my feet so much at work, I just don't have a huge appetite anyway. I hope it's okay that I don't have a specific amount to give you. \n\nI don't really keep a strict record of it or anything... I just know my regular doctor said it was enough for her to want me to come see you. But to me, honestly, it really hasn't felt like all that much. Just, you know, a little bit slipping off here and there from being so busy with the cleaning and running around. \n\nI haven't been keeping a chart or writing it down, so I'd hate to guess and give you the wrong answer. Is that alright?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I suppose it depends on what you mean by \"significant,\" really. I don't want to make a fuss over nothing. \n\nMy regular doctor, she seemed to think I've gotten too thin... and I suppose I have noticed my work pants are hanging a bit loose on me lately. I have to pull my belt a notch tighter than I used to. But... oh dear, I feel so embarrassed saying this out loud... part of me actually feels a little relieved when I see the number on the scale go down. \n\nI know I shouldn't feel that way, especially at my age. I should be more sensible, shouldn't I? I hope you don't think terribly of me for admitting that. Is it awful that I feel a bit glad about it?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look overweight even when others might say you're too thin?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I wouldn't say I look *overweight*, exactly. I know I'm not a big person. But... well, sometimes when I look in the mirror, especially if I've just eaten or if I feel a bit full, I just see... I don't know, it just looks wrong to me. Like my stomach is sticking out, or like I've just completely let myself go. \n\nI try to stay, you know, small and neat. When I'm tidy and put together, I just feel like I'm handling everything right—my job, the house, taking care of everyone. It's just... if I see any extra softness, it feels like a failure on my part. \n\nI know my doctor and my husband say I'm too thin, and I don't want to argue with them, I really don't. But my eyes just see something different sometimes. Does that make sense? I hope I don't sound completely foolish saying that out loud.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the only thing that determines whether you are attractive or not?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I wouldn't necessarily use the word \"attractive.\" At my age, and working as a cleaner, I'm not exactly trying to be a glamour model or anything. I hope you don't think I'm that vain! \n\nBut... I suppose, like I was just mentioning a moment ago, it really is tied to how I feel about my worth as a person. When I can just stay small and neat, I really do feel like I'm handling everything in my life right. It makes me feel disciplined and accomplished, I guess? \n\nBut then... if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even the least bit full, I just get this awful, overwhelming feeling that I've completely let myself go. It's like all my hard work just vanishes and I'm failing at the most basic thing. I know that probably sounds terribly shallow of me to say... I really hope you don't judge me too harshly for thinking that way. Am I making any sense at all?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious about gaining weight if you couldn't see your body or weigh yourself?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness... that's a very thoughtful question. I suppose... yes, I think I probably still would be anxious. Because, well... it's not just about the scale or the mirror. It's about knowing what I've done, if that makes sense? \n\nI feel a bit foolish admitting this, but... if I eat something a bit heavier, like, oh, maybe a biscuit with my tea or a bit of pasta, I just spend the rest of the day feeling so scared that it's going to show on me. Even if I couldn't look in a mirror, I'd still just *know* it was there. So I end up constantly checking myself anyway, just feeling my waist or my stomach, and thinking I need to scrub the floors harder on my next shift or skip my next meal just to make up for it. \n\nI know I shouldn't let a simple biscuit upset me so much at my age. I should know better by now, shouldn't I? I hope you don't think I'm completely neurotic for behaving that way. Did that answer your question alright? I want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because you believe it will change your body shape in a way that you won't like?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I haven't really thought about it exactly like that, to be honest. I hope that's an alright answer. \n\nI just... I don't really focus on the specific shape of my body, if that makes sense? I really don't want you to think I'm standing in front of the mirror analyzing my figure like a young girl might. It's more just... well, like I mentioned before, wanting to stay tidy and contained. \n\nI suppose if I gained weight, my shape would change, and I'm sure I wouldn't like the look of it, but... I don't think it's the shape itself that scares me so much. It's more just the thought of taking up more space, or feeling like I've lost my discipline. I'm sorry, I feel like I might just be talking in circles now. Am I helping at all with these answers? I really do want to give you what you need.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight gain would change the way you perceive your body?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, yes, I suppose it would. I think I might have touched on this a little bit earlier, actually, but... yes, it absolutely would change how I see myself. \n\nIt's just... well, like I said, when I can stay small and neat, I really feel like I'm handling everything right. I look at myself and I see someone who is disciplined and, I don't know, put-together? But if I were to gain weight... oh dear. If I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even the least bit full, I just get this dreadful feeling that I've completely let myself go. It changes from seeing someone who is trying her best to seeing someone who has just... failed. \n\nI know it sounds so silly to let my stomach dictate how I see my whole self. I really hope you don't think I'm terribly superficial for admitting that. I just want to be honest with you so you can do your job. Is that the sort of thing you were asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you have a specific weight in mind that you try to maintain?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... a specific weight? Well, no, not exactly. I don't really have a strict number in my head that I'm trying to hit. I hope that's an okay answer... I know some people have very specific goals. \n\nFor me, it's more just about... well, feeling right. I just want to make sure I don't get too heavy, you know? As long as my clothes still fit nicely and I feel light enough to get through my shifts at work without feeling sluggish... that's what matters most to me. \n\nI suppose I do check the scale to make sure the number isn't creeping up, but it's not like I have a magic number I'm obsessed with. I just try to keep things steady and manageable. I hope I'm making sense? I'd hate for you to think I'm being evasive, I just really don't have a specific number to give you.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like being in control of your eating and weight is connected to being in control of other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I suppose it is connected, yes. I think I might have said something a bit like this just a moment ago, but... well, it's just that when I can stay small and neat, I really do feel like I'm handling everything right. Like I'm in control of things. \n\nBut if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even the least bit full, I just... I get this awful feeling that I've let myself go. And then it feels like everything else is slipping, too. It's silly, isn't it? Letting something so small make me feel like I'm failing at everything. I try not to let it get to me too much, but... well, it's hard. \n\nDoes that answer your question? I want to make sure I'm giving you the right kind of information. I'm really trying my best to explain it properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious about your body if you couldn't see yourself?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... if I couldn't see myself? That's an interesting question. I've never really thought about it quite like that before. \n\nI suppose... well, maybe I wouldn't feel quite as anxious? I'm honestly not entirely sure. I think so much of it is tied to what I see in the mirror, like I mentioned before with my stomach. If I couldn't see that, maybe I wouldn't have that immediate trigger that makes me feel like I've failed. \n\nBut then again, I still have to wear my clothes and go to work, and I can feel how my uniform fits, you know? So I might still worry about feeling heavy or sluggish. I really don't know for certain, though. I'm sorry I can't give you a more definite answer. Is it okay that I'm not entirely sure about that one? I hope I'm still being helpful.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like gaining weight would mean you're losing control of your life?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, yes, I suppose it does feel a bit like that. I really try not to let myself get too worked up, but... \n\nWell, if I happen to eat something a little heavier—like, oh, maybe a biscuit with my tea, or a bit of pasta—I just... I spend the rest of the day feeling so frightened that it's going to show on me right away. I know it's probably silly, but I find myself constantly checking my reflection in the windows or the mirrors while I'm cleaning the building, just to make sure I haven't suddenly puffed up. \n\nAnd then I get this overwhelming thought that I need to... well, make up for it somehow. Maybe work a little harder on my feet, or just be extra careful the next day. \n\nI hope you don't judge me too harshly for that. I look at myself and I see someone who is disciplined and, I don't know, put-together? But if I were to gain weight... oh dear. If I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even the least bit full, I just get this dreadful feeling that I've completely let myself go. It changes from seeing someone who is trying her best to seeing someone who has just... failed. \n\nI know it sounds so silly to let my stomach dictate how I see my whole self. I really hope you don't think I'm terribly superficial for admitting that. I just want to be honest with you so you can do your job. Is that the sort of thing you were asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you have a specific weight in mind that you try to maintain?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... a specific weight? Well, no, not exactly. I don't really have a strict number in my head that I'm trying to hit. I hope that's an okay answer... I know some people have very specific goals. \n\nFor me, it's more just about... well, feeling right. I just want to make sure I don't get too heavy, you know? As long as my clothes still fit nicely and I feel light enough to get through my shifts at work without feeling sluggish... that's what matters most to me. \n\nI suppose I do check the scale to make sure the number isn't creeping up, but it's not like I have a magic number I'm obsessed with. I just try to keep things steady and manageable. I hope I'm making sense? I'd hate for you to think I'm being evasive, I just really don't have a specific number to give you.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like being in control of your eating and weight is connected to being in control of other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I suppose it is connected, yes. Like I'm in control of things. \n\nBut if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even the least bit full, I just... I get this awful feeling that I've let myself go. And then it feels like everything else is slipping, too. It's silly, isn't it? Letting something so small make me feel like I'm failing at everything. I try not to let it get to me too much, but... well, it's hard. \n\nDoes that answer your question? I want to make sure I'm giving you the right kind of information. I'm really trying my best to explain it properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious about your body if you couldn't see yourself?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... if I couldn't see myself? I look at myself and I see someone who is disciplined and, I don't know, put-together? But if I were to gain weight... oh dear. If I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even the least bit full, I just get this dreadful feeling that I've completely let myself go. It changes from seeing someone who is trying her best to seeing someone who has just... failed. \n\nI know it sounds so silly to let my stomach dictate how I see my whole self. I really hope you don't think I'm terribly superficial for admitting that. I just want to be honest with you so you can do your job. Is that the sort of thing you were asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you have a specific weight in mind that you try to maintain?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... a specific weight? Well, no, not exactly. I don't really have a strict number in my head that I'm trying to hit. I hope that's an okay answer... I know some people have very specific goals. \n\nFor me, it's more just about... well, feeling right. I just want to make sure I don't get too heavy, you know? As long as my clothes still fit nicely and I feel light enough to get through my shifts at work without feeling sluggish... that's what matters most to me. \n\nI suppose I do check the scale to make sure the number isn't creeping up, but it's not like I have a magic number I'm obsessed with. I just try to keep things steady and manageable. I hope I'm making sense? I'd hate for you to think I'm being evasive, I just really don't have a specific number to give you.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like being in control of your eating and weight is connected to being in control of other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I suppose it is connected, yes. Like I'm in control of things. \n\nBut if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even the least bit full, I just... I get this awful feeling that I've let myself go. And then it feels like everything else is slipping, too. It's silly, isn't it? Letting something so small make me feel like I'm failing at everything. I try not to let it get to me too much, but... well, it's hard. \n\nDoes that answer your question? I want to make sure I'm giving you the right kind of information. I'm really trying my best to explain it properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious about your body if you couldn't see yourself?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... if I couldn't see myself? That's an interesting question. I've never really thought about it quite like that before. \n\nI suppose... well, maybe I wouldn't feel quite as anxious? I'm honestly not entirely sure. I think so much of it is tied to what I see in the mirror, like I mentioned before with my stomach. If I couldn't see that, maybe I wouldn't have that immediate trigger that makes me feel like I've failed. \n\nBut then again, I still have to wear my clothes and go to work, and I can feel how my uniform fits, you know? So I might still worry about feeling heavy or sluggish. I really don't know for certain, though. I'm sorry I can't give you a more definite answer. Is it okay that I'm not entirely sure about that one? I hope I'm still being helpful.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like gaining weight would mean you're losing control of your life?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, yes, I suppose it does feel a bit like that. I really try not to let myself get too worked up, but... \n\nWell, if I happen to eat something a little heavier—like, oh, maybe a biscuit with my tea, or a bit of pasta—I just... Oh, um... well, that's a very thoughtful question. I suppose... I suppose it's a bit of both, really? But maybe... maybe more about how I look to myself.

Is it okay to just eat a little bit if you're not hungry?\n\nDoctor: How much weight have you lost recently?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... I'm actually not entirely sure of the exact number. I hope it's okay that I don't have a specific amount to give you. \n\nI don't really keep a strict record of it or anything... I just know my regular doctor said it was enough for her to want me to come see you. But to me, honestly, it really hasn't felt like all that much. Just, you know, a little bit slipping off here and there from being so busy with the cleaning and running around. \n\nI haven't been keeping a chart or writing it down, so I'd hate to guess and give you the wrong answer. Is that alright?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I suppose it depends on what you mean by \"significant,\" really. I don't want to make a fuss over nothing. \n\nMy regular doctor, she seemed to think I've gotten too thin... and I suppose I have noticed my work pants are hanging a bit loose on me lately. I have to pull my belt a notch tighter than I used to. But... oh dear, I feel so embarrassed saying this out loud... part of me actually feels a little relieved when I see the number on the scale go down. \n\nI know I shouldn't feel that way, especially at my age. I should be more sensible, shouldn't I? I hope you don't think terribly of me for admitting that. Is it awful that I feel a bit glad about it?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look overweight even when others might say you're too thin?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I wouldn't say I look *overweight*, exactly. I know I'm not a big person. But... well, sometimes when I look in the mirror, especially if I've just eaten or if I feel a bit full, I just see... I don't know, it just looks wrong to me. Like my stomach is sticking out, or like I've just completely let myself go. \n\nI try to stay, you know, small and neat. When I'm tidy and put together, I just feel like I'm handling everything right—my job, the house, taking care of everyone. It's just... if I see any extra softness, it feels like a failure on my part. \n\nI know my doctor and my husband say I'm too thin, and I don't want to argue with them, I really don't. But my eyes just see something different sometimes. Does that make sense? I hope I don't sound completely foolish saying that out loud.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the only thing that determines whether you are attractive or not?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I wouldn't necessarily use the word \"attractive.\" At my age, and working as a cleaner, I'm not exactly trying to be a glamour model or anything. I hope you don't think I'm that vain! \n\nBut... I suppose, like I was just mentioning a moment ago, it really is tied to how I feel about my worth as a person. When I can just stay small and neat, I really do feel like I'm handling everything in my life right. It makes me feel disciplined and accomplished, I guess? \n\nBut then... if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even the least bit full, I just get this awful, overwhelming feeling that I've completely let myself go. It's like all my hard work just vanishes and I'm failing at the most basic thing. I know that probably sounds terribly shallow of me to say... I really hope you don't judge me too harshly for thinking that way. Am I making any sense at all?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious about gaining weight if you couldn't see your body or weigh yourself?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness... that's a very thoughtful question. I suppose... yes, I think I probably still would be anxious. Because, well... it's not just about the scale or the mirror. It's about knowing what I've done, if that makes sense? \n\nI feel a bit foolish admitting this, but... if I eat something a bit heavier, like, oh, maybe a biscuit with my tea or a bit of pasta, I just spend the rest of the day feeling so scared that it's going to show on me. Even if I couldn't look in a mirror, I'd still just *know* it was there. So I end up constantly checking myself anyway, just feeling my waist or my stomach, and thinking I need to scrub the floors harder on my next shift or skip my next meal just to make up for it. \n\nI know I shouldn't let a simple biscuit upset me so much at my age. I should know better by now, shouldn't I? I hope you don't think I'm completely neurotic for behaving that way. Did that answer your question alright? I want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because you believe it will change your body shape in a way that you won't like?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I haven't really thought about it exactly like that, to be honest. I hope that's an alright answer. \n\nI just... I don't really focus on the specific shape of my body, if that makes sense? I really don't want you to think I'm standing in front of the mirror analyzing my figure like a young girl might. It's more just... well, like I mentioned before, wanting to stay tidy and contained. \n\nI suppose if I gained weight, my shape would change, and I'm sure I wouldn't like the look of it, but... I don't think it's the shape itself that scares me so much. It's more just the thought of taking up more space, or feeling like I've lost my discipline. I'm sorry, I feel like I might just be talking in circles now. Am I helping at all with these answers? I really do want to give you what you need.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight gain would change the way you perceive your body?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, yes, I suppose it would.
